@@108698312 <h> The late Bill Wood was a great Canberran who did much <p> I was saddened to learn of the death of Bill Wood . I had the privilege and pleasure of serving Bill for several years as director ACT Environment and Conservation and acting CEO of the Department of Environment , Land and Planning . <h> Subscribe now for unlimited access . <p> Bill respected and valued the role of the public service and encouraged public servants to be forthright , even when that advice created difficulties for him . He applied a critical mind but was prepared to act independently of the advice when he felt the need to . He was well-respected by his officials . <p> One of his key achievements as environment minister was establishing the ACT Commissioner of the Environment as an independent statutory office in 1993 . ( Supplied ) <p> Known as the ' godfather of African cinema ' , Ousmane Semb ? ne began making films at a time when depictions of the continent and its people were solely the purview of Hollywood and white ethnographers . In French colonies such as Semb ? ne 's native Senegal , Black Africans had been prohibited from making films by law : from 1934 to 1960 , when France relinquished its colonial holdings , the Laval Decree ensured that cinema could not be put in the service of any subversive , anti-colonial cause . <p> Having already made a name for himself as an author , Semb ? ne turned to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ His 1966 debut feature , the deceptively slender Black Girl ( it 's not quite an hour long ) , relates the story of a young Senegalese woman who travels to the Cote d'Azur to work for a white couple . It 's an elegant but brutal allegory for the perils of neocolonialism , and anticipates Get Out in its incisive take-down of ' progressive ' white liberals . <p> The film 's merits have only recently begun to be properly recognised in the west , after Semb ? ne 's death in 2007 . The filmmaker may well not have cared about this belated praise , however : Asked in the 1980s whether he thought European audiences understood his films , he replied , " Why be a sunflower and turn towards the sun ? I myself am the sun . " KY . <p> Director-writer Jaydon Martin says Flathead is about " that guy you see at the end of the bar " . @@108802788 <p> The Israeli-Palestinian conflict matters more to Australians and Indonesians than either country 's foreign policy elite previously acknowledged . So , Canberra and Jakarta should cooperate to help resolve it . They should articulate a joint vision of the two-state solution and build international support for it . That could encourage , shape and shorten future Palestinian-Israeli negotiations . <p> The cliche that no one can want peace more than Israelis and Palestinians was never really true . The conflict 's global impact is now obvious . It 's straining social cohesion in Australia and inflaming public opinion in Indonesia ( and in Malaysia and even Singapore ) . Much of the Global South sees the question of Palestine as Exhibit A of Western double standards . Whether or not it 's true , that view makes it harder to defend the rules-based order , from Ukraine to the South China Sea . Because Yemen 's Houthis purport to be fighting for Palestine , efforts to counter their attacks on commercial shipping in the Red Sea and Indian Ocean have received minimal international support . <p> More states have a stake in resolving the Israeli-Palestinian conflict , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ largely because Washington still dominates the process . Instead , most countries have focused their diplomatic energy on symbolic action at the UN and inconsequential rhetoric . The fact that conservative Arab states still routinely denounce Israel while quietly cooperating with it underscores the dysfunction in that division of labour . <p> Middle powers , including Indonesia and Australia , should work with the few positive trends that have emerged over the past two decades : more states willing to recognise Israel ; more states willing to recognise Palestine ; more states interested in Israeli-Palestinian peace ; and more details added to the proposed two-state solution . <p> US-led peacemaking might be faltering , but since the 1990s it has delivered some genuine breakthroughs . In 2000 , President Clinton 's Camp David summit collapsed in failure but it broke taboos and set parameters for continued Track 1 and Track 2 negotiations . Since 2020 , the Abraham Accords have normalised relations between Israel and several Arab states , even as they sidelined the Palestinian issue . <p> Saudi Arabia will be key to future efforts . Riyadh was readying @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . But it 's now conditioning recognition -- and cooperation on Gaza -- on tangible and irreversible Israeli steps towards establishing a Palestinian state . That 's a revival of Saudi Arabia 's 2002 take-it-or-leave-it offer to Israel : recognition in return for a full withdrawal from the territories occupied in 1967 . Riyadh should refine that blunt demand , drawing on Israeli-Palestinian negotiations since then to detail necessary compromises on both sides . <p> Saudi Arabia need not act alone . It could be backed by the Arab League or even the G20 . Most G20 countries can now agree on many more details of the two-state solution than they could when the group first met in 1999 . They could concur that the border should be based on the pre-1967 lines and , possibly , on the basis for territorial swaps ; on demilitarisation of the Palestinian state ; on Palestinian refugees being settled in the Palestinian state ( rather than Israel ) ; and on some special arrangements for Jerusalem . <p> Australia and Indonesia could bridge the gaps . They 've often been on opposing sides @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ April , Foreign Minister Wong raised the possibility of Australia recognising Palestinian statehood ' as a way of building momentum towards a two-state solution ' . Before 7 October , Indonesia was quietly seeking to establish diplomatic relations with Israel . As defence minister , president-elect Prabowo Subianto led earlier efforts to normalise relations . <p> Like the Arab signatories to the Abraham Accords , Indonesia has pragmatic interests in ties with Israel . In particular , Jakarta 's bid to join the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development will ultimately require the assent of all existing members , including Israel . OECD Secretary-General Mathias Cormann , an Australian , has reportedly sought to broker ' a commitment from Indonesia to soften its stance on Israel in exchange for Israel waiving its objection ' . But Indonesian recognition of Israel would now be domestically toxic . <p> Instead , Canberra should work with Jakarta to outline a joint vision of the two-state solution . This would see Australia detail the Palestinian state it proposes to recognise and Indonesia set out realistic conditions for recognising Israel . The potential diplomatic win @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ An Australian-Indonesian proposal could provide the nucleus for one endorsed by a larger group of states , perhaps including most members of the G20 or even the OECD . <p> How much difference would that make ? It 's true that a peace agreement looks further away than ever . The Palestinian Authority is weak and divided . Israeli opposition to Palestinian statehood seems firmer than ever . But Israeli and Palestinian politics have always been shaped by the international environment . And both sides need international support : the Palestinians to build a state and Israel to protect itself from Iran and Iran 's proxies . <p> The impact of a middle-power peace initiative will depend on the work that countries such as Australia and Indonesia are willing to put into it . <p> Author <p> Ben Scott is a senior advisor at the ANU National Security College . <p> I published a 2023 study showing that because of this fact , a fire can be hugely destructive even though modelled as low severity . <p> The 2022 Project Vesta ( CSIRO and DBCA ) findings were that unless plants were burning ( called ' Phase II ' ) , flames were small and of low-intensity . <p> The claim that doubling the fuel load doubles the rate of spread came from a 1967 leaflet showing nine data points and was never peer-reviewed . <p> It was later tested in a peer-reviewed study , which concluded that there was no relationship between fuel load and rate of spread . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be concerned about was instead the understorey , which forms only a small part of the fuel load . <p> My claim about low flammability in very long-unburnt forests comes from publishing several peer-reviewed analyses of government records , and my knowledge of burning sub-alpine forests comes from years of actually doing it and seeing the results . <p> We ca n't stop our learning halfway through last century if we want to keep our forests . <h> Dr Philip Zylstra , adjunct associate professor , Curtin University <h> No defence , Peter <p> Apropos Peter Costello 's physical contact with the journalist at the Canberra Airport , he argued that he did n't lift a finger or fist to the aggrieved journo . <p> This may be so , however , he did seem to " shoulder bump " him . <p> The journo was walking backward , and allegedly fell over an advertising board . <p> If Mr Costello maintains such defence did he step over both journalist and advertising board while continuing his walk ? @@109001897 <p> For 8 months , images and videos of the carnage in Gaza have been flooding social media . They give a powerful view of what 's happening on the ground -- but they also have the potential to cause vicarious trauma . <p> Today -- what is vicarious trauma , and why doomscrolling does n't help . <p> Guests : <p> Dr Arash **30;0;TOOLONG of the Stress , Trauma , and Anxiety Research Clinic Author , Afraid : Understanding the Purpose of Fear , and Harnessing the Power of Anxiety <p> Professor Kim FelminghamChair of Clinical Psychology , Melbourne School of Psychological Sciences University of Melbourne <p> Credits : <p> Presenter/Producer : Sana Qadar <p> Producer : Rose Kerr <p> Sound engineer : Simon Branthwaite <h> Credits <p> Sana Qadar : Depending on the social media silo that you 're in at the moment , your Insta or TikTok or Twitter might be business as usual . And normally we , the audience watching news coverage of a war zone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on . The most graphic scenes would be blurred or would never make it to air . And what people are filming and posting from Gaza in particular , feels unlike anything that 's hit Instagram or TikTok or Twitter before . <p> Social media video : If death had a smell I 'm smelling it right now . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : You 're seeing it from the perspective of the victims survivors here . <p> Dr Arash Javanbakht : So there is absolute vulnerability that is being seen . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : I think the other thing is the impact on children . <p> Sana Qadar : On the one hand , these videos have given us a powerful view of what 's happening on the ground , one we would n't otherwise get , as Israel has mostly banned foreign journalists from entering Gaza . It 's not just one time of watching those videos . <p> Sana Qadar : To be clear , this is n't just a risk for people watching content from Gaza . There 's been some incredibly harrowing videos released of the October 7th attacks as well , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risk , too . So today , the origins of vicarious trauma , how social media increases the risk , and how the current violence in the Middle East is magnifying all of that . <p> Dr Arash Javanbakht : We feel heartless . We feel like I 'm not doing enough if I 'm not following this , but I want to break out some news . Me tormenting myself , I 'm going through that video ten times is not going to help the person who suffered in that video , or the other people who are suffering compared to when I went through it one time . <p> Sana Qadar : This is All in the Mind . I 'm Sana Qadar . Long before the concept of vicarious trauma was ever applied to social media . It was noted first in people like therapists and social workers and first responders , people who see and hear awful things day in , day out . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : Yeah . Look , I think it really did start with frontline staff , first responders . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of PTSD really did n't emerge until the 1980s from Vietnam veterans . You know , we always had other ideas or labels like war neurosis and things like that from First World War and Second World War . So the whole history of PTSD really came about from the military context . <p> Sana Qadar : This is Professor Kim Felmingham . She 's the chair of clinical psychology at the Melbourne School of Psychological Sciences at the University of Melbourne . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : I think then there was a gradually recognition and realisation that PTSD can emerge from sexual assault from a range of other experiences , childhood abuse . But I think it was n't till later that people really started to recognise actually severe PTSD and trauma can also occur from the cumulative observation or exposure to traumatic experiences , even if someone 's not directly the victim or experiencing that trauma themselves . <p> Sana Qadar : So people like police officers turning up at violent crime scenes . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : Absolutely . Even , you know , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who are watching or exposed to images and videos of violence in particular , were flagged as people who can be developing vicarious trauma . <p> Sana Qadar : And so what is vicarious trauma ? Is it essentially PTSD ? Just , you know , the fact that you were n't specifically involved in what happened ? What is it ? <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : Vicarious trauma is probably a broader definition . It is having trauma reactions or trauma responses when you are witnessing something , a trauma , but not directly present . You know , increased stress , difficulty sleeping , increased irritability , negative cognitions about yourself and the world and others . So that can be a real damage to your ideas of trusting others or how safe the world can feel . If people are developing PTSD . It may also be that they 're getting sort of intrusive images or memories or flashbacks of some of the images they have observed . So I think the reactions people have to that vicarious traumatisation can be quite variable and individual and vary @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Sana Qadar : And when did we first start to see or understand that media could cause vicarious trauma , like is 9/11 an early example the way that was covered ? <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : Yeah , absolutely . And I think the difference there was it was the level of media is saturation . <p> News anchor : The twin towers of the World Trade Centre have collapsed after being hit by two American airliners hijacked earlier . <p> Professor Kim Felmingham : You know , really what we were running , there was a 24 hour news cycle of September 11th , you know , and you just could not really escape the footage . For the first time in the party 's history it won a second Senate seat in every state of Australia , taking its representation in the red chamber to a record high of 12 seats ( though they 've since lost one after Lidia Thorpe defected to the crossbench ) . <p> But more significantly , after 12 long years as the party 's only MP in the lower house , party leader Adam Bandt was joined by three new colleagues in the green chamber as the Greens swept the Queensland seats of Griffith , Ryan and Brisbane . <p> In the lead-up to that election , a then-member of the Morrison cabinet confidently told me that if teal candidates were standing in the northern capital , the Liberals might have been in trouble -- but because the alternative was either a Greens or Labor candidate , they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The next election could be even more consequential for the balance-of-power party . The Greens are targeting a long list of lower house seats ( that 's nothing new ) and their ambition should not be lightly dismissed . <p> Consider the 2022 results . The Greens targeted five Labor-held seats -- Griffith in Queensland , Richmond in NSW , Canberra in the ACT , and Macnamara and Wills in Victoria . And they had four held by the Liberals -- Queensland 's Brisbane and Ryan and Victoria 's Kooyong and Higgins -- in their sights . Five of those nine seats fell , three to the Greens and two to the teals . A similar result is possible this time . <p> And there are five reasons why the party 's strategists believe this could be the Greens ' most successful election yet . <p> Loading <p> The first reason is that Labor is in government now . Before 2022 , the Greens ' most successful election was 2010 , when Bandt entered the lower house and the party claimed six Senate seats for the first time . This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ re-election while the Greens were a potent , organised political force -- and they won over voters who were disappointed with the Rudd-Gillard government . Picture Getty Images <p> Tens of thousands of people are confirmed to have been killed , a number which is certainly much higher , with the United States Special Envoy for Sudan estimating up to 150,000 civilians killed . Eighteen million people are acutely hungry and 3.6 million children acutely malnourished . It is a war disproportionately affecting the next generation , with a quarter of those displaced being under five years old . <p> Australia was absent at the recent Paris Conference which marked the anniversary of the crisis in April . With our allies making commitments commensurate with the scale of need , Australia 's absence feeds into a longstanding narrative of Africa not being our problem , or too far away . <p> This may be one of the biggest humanitarian catastrophes we see in a generation , and Australia has not come to the table on it this year . <p> This hits home for the 13,000 Australian Sudanese diaspora living in Australia , who are urgently demanding humanitarian assistance from their government to help alleviate the suffering @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> While this community appreciated the $20.5 million humanitarian package committed by the Australian government for Sudan in 2023 , much more is necessary to meet the escalating needs . The diaspora expects a response that reflects the boundless generosity of the Australian public , known for their compassionate support towards global humanitarian crises . <p> The diaspora sees Australia 's potential to lead by example in this moment and provide substantial relief whilst also emphasising the deep moral obligation to support those who are suffering profoundly , despite where they may live in the world . <p> Africa receives the highest amount of funding through members of the Australian Council for International Development globally , accounting for almost a quarter of all funds to international projects ( 24.2 per cent ) . <p> Data for financial year 2021-22 reveals 625 international projects received a total $267.6 million , across 32 African countries delivered through 44 Australian NGOs . A third of this funding was contributed by donations , other income , followed by non-monetary donations . <p> With ambition for another United Nations Security Council seat , this government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prioritise humanitarian need where it is greatest , right now , in Sudan . <p> On the ground in Sudan today , families share horrific stories of their daily struggles , marked by a severe lack of medical supplies , food , and safety . Accounts of innocent and vulnerable people living in slave-like conditions are all too common . <p> Those in internally displaced camps in Darfur face unimaginable hardships , with many unable to cope with constantly spending their days burying young children and women who have died from starvation , all while avoiding the next militant attack . <p> The Sudanese diaspora and the humanitarian community in Australia is desperate for this government to provide the urgent assistance needed to save lives and restore some semblance of hope and security for those left behind . <p> Naomi Brooks is an Australian Council for International Development humanitarian advisor and Amad Mohamed is from the Sudanese Australian Advocacy Network .

Five years ago , it would n't have noteworthy at all . It felt retro , like making a call on a push-button handset or sending a fax . We 've become so conditioned to paying electronically that cash itself is becoming an endangered species . <p> In 2007 , about 70 per cent of all transactions were settled in cash . These days , it 's 13 per cent . And while it seems convenient and more hygienic to wave the plastic over the eftpos machine , we forget that businesses are entitled to extract a surcharge - and many more are doing just that as money gets tighter . The rules state that they must n't charge more than it costs them to process the transaction . And businesses are obliged to display a notice telling you of the surcharge . <p> The surcharges are small - between 0.5 and 1.5 per cent for each transaction - but add them up over time and you could be spending significant amounts to spend your money . Eight cents on top of the fiver you pay for your latte . Seventeen cents on your $11 pint at the pub @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a day during the working week could end up costing you $20.80 - slightly more than the lobster I fished out of my wallet . <p> The $20 bill had sat in my wallet for so long . Picture Shutterstock <p> The move towards a cashless economy seems inevitable but resistance is growing . At the beginning of June he introduced a private member 's bill which would make it unlawful for businesses not to accept cash payments . The bill 's chances of success are remote . @@109625937 <h> The Weekend Mumbo : Radio ? Politics ? Media Watch ? Where to next for Julian Assange ? <p> On Thursday night , people planning to settle in for a double episode of Law and Order SVU on Ten were met not with the familiar sight of Ice-T solving crimes while pretending he did n't write a song called ' Cop Killer ' once , but instead by a boy with a blonde bowl haircut , hacking into the type of fluoro-fonted , black-screen databases that only exist in the Matrix . <p> Yes , Julian Assange is back in Australia , a free man after copping a plea deal for time spent in the company of Pamela Anderson -- and he is everywhere at the moment . Everyone has caught Assange fever , it seems . Despite his wife 's reasonable assertion he needs time to " let our family be a family " , we 're all waiting to see what he 'll do next . Aside from a double thumbs-up . <p> Swept up in the wave , Ten dug up an old telemovie from 2012 , produced during an earlier rush of Assange fever @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ LaPaglia brothers . Curiously , this film focuses not on Assange 's recent trials , but on a teenage Julian , back when he was a computer hacker being chased by a bumbling Australian Federal Police force whose idea of sophisticated computing power back in 1989 was the Game Boy . It 's a good film , and a good reminder of Assange 's various saleable skills . <p> These days , in the eyes of the general public , computer hackers are ranked somewhere between murderers and parking inspectors , due mostly to a flood of recent attacks on institutions like Optus , Telstra , Medibank , et al . that basically means the entire Australian population has now had personal data stolen and sold -- by hackers . Even if it 's a boat licence number , it still feels like an invasion . So , I get the public sentiment against hackers -- and against murderers and parking inspectors for that matter . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> But the type of ' hacking ' Assange ascribed to -- at least before he broke The Espionage Act -- is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ into a system just to prove you can ; to look around , and see what 's inside . ' Look but do n't steal ' is the credo . Like bored kids breaking into a mall but not stealing anything , I guess . There is also an educational element , in seeing how sophisticated systems are built , and -- no doubt -- a thrill in gaining unauthorised access . Oftentimes these hackers will stumble across a major flaw and inform the organisation of the pending danger , like one 15-year-old Aussie kid who hacked the UN and discovered " around 100,000 highly sensitive records that could have been weaponised if it fell into the wrong hands . " Gulp . <p> Taking the ' if you ca n't beat ' em , hire ' em ' approach , Fortune 500 companies now routinely pay these hackers big money to test and expose flaws in their security systems . There 's a US startup called Synack with around 1,000 hackers on its books , who can earn millions in bounties and payments for short-term hacking jobs . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 2022 ; the following year he was the keynote speaker at Scotland 's annual tech industry meetup . Years earlier , a kid who published step-by-step instructions on how to hack your own PlayStation 3 to play pirated CDs was snapped up by Zuckerberg , and put to work at Facebook . <p> So , Assange could do a bit of remote hacking for some pocket money , as he sorts out his next move . <p> There 's also Wikileaks , remember ? <p> This hacking side-hustle might also be a good funding model for Wikileaks : some weekend hacking work to fund the flagging organisation ? That 's assuming Assange has any interest in picking back up . <p> The website lays dormant , quaintly cast in Internet 1.0 carbonine , looking like a turn-of-the-century Geocities site rather than the home base of an organisation that took on various arms of the US Government . <p> Last December , as he was preparing for his final fight for freedom , a journalist from The Nation visited Assange in Belmarsh prison and asked about the roadblocks for Wikileaks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ journalist Charles Glass paints a friendship between the pair , but uses no direct quotes , instead describing the thrust of their conversation , as if retelling it later at the pub . In fact , the only Assange quote used in the entire piece is " they call it prison pale " , a comment regarding his ghostly complexion . But despite the lack of actual quotes , the below exchange aptly shows the hassles , legal and fiscal , that Wikileaks faces in 2024 : <p> With the invasions of Ukraine and Gaza , I say , now is an important time for whistleblowers to send documents to WikiLeaks . He regrets that WikiLeaks is no longer able to expose war crimes and corruption as in the past . His imprisonment and US government surveillance and restrictions on WikiLeaks ' funding wards off potential whistleblowers . He fears that other media outlets are not filling the vacuum . <p> So , no funding , government surveillance , and that pesky prison sentence . All decent-sized roadblocks . Wikileaks still has reach though , should he want to fight @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ celebrity , Wikileaks ' Twitter/X profile still boasts 5.7 million followers , even if the inhumane treatment of Assange will ward off potential whistleblowers , as he points out . But , as we discussed , Assange is/was a world-class computer hacker . Get those documents yourself , Assange , like you did as a pesky teen ! <p> There 's also the political route to consider . Assange for PM ? After all , one of the storylines that 's fallen by the wayside somewhat over the years is Assange 's failed Senate bid back in 2013 , where he launched a Wikileaks political party from the Ecuadorean embassy , at one point posting a Farnham parody video pointing fun at Tony Abbott , Kevin Rudd and Julia Gillard . <p> In fact , he only launched the party after taking umbrage at Julia Gillard labelling Wikileaks " illegal " , despite advice to the contrary from the AFP . He basically mounted a political party out of spite . <p> Of his political leanings , Assange said he " could be described as a libertarian " , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of understanding before acting " , as he puts it . <p> Assange noted " very little difference between Liberal and Labor -- especially once they get into government . Labor suffers more from cronyism , while the Liberals care more for big business " . <p> He slammed the government for " the betrayal of the rights and interests of people by political insiders , operating in their own interests " and wished to fight for " right of citizens to live their lives free from state interference " . <p> He also said he would be " a fierce defender of press freedom ... even though I have known only too well smear by unethical media " ( which sounds quite Shakespearean ) and would " absolutely " use parliamentary privilege to squash court-imposed gag orders . <p> Sounds like a contender . Of course , the Wikileaks party took just 0.62% of the national vote in 2013 , with Assange taking 1.18% in Victoria , but as he cheerily pointed out to the ABC following the failed bid : " We are the second largest vote count @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which had a billion bucks behind it . I think that 's a pretty good outcome . " <p> So do I. But that was 11 years ago . Since then , Assange is arguably in a much better and more prominent position to make such a run . I imagine his debating skills might have softened , given he spent 15 years mostly in isolation , but I would love Assange in the political ring again , trading barbs with Barnaby , making mutton of Dutton . But it 's a long shot . <p> So , how about radio ? A medium that has been in decline since the 1960s , yet boasts the highest paid media personalities in the country . It 's immediate , it commands a huge audience . I 'm not suggesting Fifi , Hughesy , and Julian in the morning -- although would n't that be something ? But , surely Assange could score a 2GB contract to rival , if not Kyle and Jackie O 's FM windfall , then at least Ray Hadley 's $3.5 million a year . Assange 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ airwaves , and I 'd love to hear him discussing traffic congestion solutions with Alistair from Parramatta . Also , imagine the sizzle at the end of each talk-break : " After Kings Of Leon , we expose war atrocities in a little-known region . Stay locked in . " <p> He may be slightly hamstrung if he wants to operate in Australia without fear or favour , given our defamation laws are quite crippling ( something Assange could sort out as PM ) . But the US does n't seem to be a smart option , either , despite the proliferation of 24/7 talk back news channels that would jump at the chance to add him to their roster of crazies . @@109655279 <p> Terrorism is a major concern for authorities ahead of the Paris Olympic Games , with experts warning the global sporting event could attract a " diverse " range of security risks . <p> As millions prepare to flock to the French capital in July , the country remains at its highest level of security alert - initially raised back in March . <p> " Terrorism will be front and centre as a threat that 's being considered , " Trevor Easley , a major event security specialist and Executive Advisor for Alexander James &amp; Partners , told news.com.au . <p> " ( France ) has experienced terrorist incidents in crowded places in the past , with the tragic 2015 attacks . So protecting crowded venues as well as the Olympic family from a terrorist <p> attack is going to be a priority for the security authorities , " said Mr Easley , who planned and delivered security design and led the multi-agency security assurance program for the London 2012 Olympic and Paralympic Games . <p> " I would almost certainly be confident in saying that the authorities will be planning for a reasonable worst-case scenario based on their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of police Laurent Nunez singled out Islamist terrorism as " our main concern " . <p> " There is no clear-cut threat yet against the Games and our country but I 'd like to remind you that at the end of May , two individuals were arrested in Saint-Etienne and were plotting a project aimed directly at the Olympic Games , " he told a press conference last week . <p> Islamist terrorism is the main concern for authorities . Picture : Chesnot/Getty <p> Last month , an 18-year-old man was arrested in southern France on suspicion of planning an attack in the name of Islamic State at a soccer stadium to be used during the Olympics . <p> A 16-year-old was earlier arrested in April , after he allegedly announced plans to create an explosive belt and die a martyr at the Games . <p> Why the Olympics is an attractive target for terrorists <p> Like other global sporting events , the Olympic Games can be an appealing target for terrorists as it guarantees mass crowds and a high level of media exposure . <p> In 1972 , hundreds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fell victim to a terrorist attack led by the Palestinian militant organisation Black September . <p> At the time , gunmen stormed the Olympic village and took a group of Israeli athletes hostage in a tragedy that led to the deaths of 11 members of Israel 's Olympic team and a policeman . <p> " The Olympics are a massive global platform , " Dr Ram ? n Spaaij , a professor of sociology , specialising in violence , at Victoria University told news.com.au . <p> " It 's really the opportunity for live broadcasting of terror and for using that media coverage to draw attention ... It amplifies the messaging that they 're ( the terrorists groups ) are trying to get across but does n't mean it leads to sympathy for the cause . " <p> Dr Spaaij said the Olympic Games are also a symbolic target as they provide terrorists with the opportunity to " humiliate " an enemy government . <p> " The hosting of sporting events is an opportunity for countries or cities to demonstrate how well-organised they are , and how good they are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ symbol of their power . So what these attacks are trying to do is essentially to inflict damage on the government . Both physical damage , but also reputational damage . " <p> Olympic Games can be an appealing target for terrorists as it guarantees mass crowds and a high level of media exposure . Picture : Chesnot/Getty <p> A ' volatile ' political climate <p> Tensions are currently running high in France in the lead-up to the country 's landmark election , taking place over two rounds on June 30 and July 7 . <p> President Emmanuel Macron called the snap legislative elections amid the fallout from this month 's EU parliament poll and the success of far-right party National Rally . <p> Earlier this month , hundreds of thousands of people took to the streets across the country to protest the rise of the far right . <p> Dr Spaaij said the election , as well as the wider geopolitical climate , including the ongoing wars in Ukraine and Gaza , only adds to the vulnerability of the event . <p> " Generally , we know if there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a society , that is going to shape the opportunity for violent protests . " <p> Security risks and challenges <p> For the first time in Olympic history , the Opening Ceremony will be held outside along the iconic Seine , with 10,000 athletes set to sail along a 6km stretch of the river . <p> Dr Spaaij said the decision to forgo a stadium for an outdoor format invites a number of security challenges . <p> " It 's not a controlled environment as such , " he said . <p> " Access points is a big issue because they are vulnerable to being targeted , and also exit points . If something does go wrong , crowds need to be able to get out as well . " <p> To minimise the risk , free access to the ceremony will be invitation-only , and the number of spectators has been cut to about 300,000 . <p> " To manage crowd movement , we ca n't tell everyone to come , " Interior Minister G ? rald Darmanin said in March . " For security reasons that everyone understands @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are obliged to make it free but contained . " <p> Mr Easley said the outdoor format is a " whole new approach " . <p> " There is no Olympic precedent for this , " he explained . <p> " But again , they 're very experienced in major events and will have well a co-ordinated and integrated security architecture between the government security services and policing services and other key security stakeholders . " <p> The opening ceremony will be held along the Seine River . Picture : Bertrand Guay/AFP <p> Among the security risks , Mr Easley said there would also be a " high expectation " that cyber-attacks will target the Games -- as was the case with the 2018 PyeongChang Winter Olympics , which temporarily disabled internet access and telecasts . <p> " Cyber security will be a considered risk , certainly with the evolution of AI and the evolving cyber threat capabilities , " he said . <p> " I envisage the main cyber threats will be from threat actors such as nation states , organised crime , and cyber activists . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ measures to prevent or limit the <p> impacts from such attacks . " <p> Mr Easley said terrorism will be a priority for authorities . Picture : Supplied to news.com.au <p> Mr Easley said there is also a potential risk of the Olympics being used as a protest platform . <p> " More recently in Paris , we 've seen an increase in protest activity as a result of geopolitical situations such as the wars in the Middle East and Ukraine . " <p> " The security and policing authorities will almost certainly be considering their approach to peaceful assembly , as well as non-peaceful assembly as part of Games time security planning . " <p> To further ensure the safety of spectres and athletes , a large army camp is being constructed and 18,000 military personnel , as well as 45,000 police and internal security forces will be deployed to Paris . <p> " It 's more the smaller scale attacks , from a professional perspective , that I 'd be most concerned about , " he explained . <p> " What 's important to remember is it can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be focused on let 's say only the Islamic State or thinking the threat comes exclusively from large organised groups planning really big attacks , quite often it 's the more smaller scale . <p> " There could be individuals inspired by these kind of ideologies , or individuals that have sort of non ideological grievances . " <p> Dr Spaaij said smaller scale attacks , which could involve stabbings with knives , could take place in areas around Olympic venues or surrounding areas around France , which are easier to access than an Olympic sites . <p> " That 's the trend at the moment . Smaller-scale attacks could happen in different locations , and it 's often kind of the ' do it yourself ' ( attacks ) with a knife or a van . It 's very rarely ever firearms , because they 're generally quite hard to get . Picture : Dimitar Dilkoff/AFP <p> Mr Easley said Paris would have likely spent years preparing security for the Olympics and stressed those attending should not be concerned . <p> " Spectators and the Olympic family are going to see a different range of security forces and measures .
